The Living Voice — Engineering Brief
· 

Engineering Brief·The Shelf·Ingram Manor LLC

15 August 2026·2026-08-15·Rev. A

Handoff · the reciter, the certificate, and what the emerald is for

TheLiving Voice

Every spell before this one is about the dead

One hundred and ninety spells, and every one of them is spoken
for the dead — recited over the body, written on the coffin, addressed to
gatekeepers on behalf of somebody who can no longer speak. The reading room holds the
same arrangement: a thousand figures, every one of them silent, their words preserved
and unable to be uttered by the mouths that wrote them.

Read-aloud inverts it. A living person puts breath through a
dead person's sentence. That is not a bonus mechanic bolted to a quiz. It is the only
place in this entire system where the direction of the transaction reverses — where
the seeker gives something to the record rather than taking something from it.

This brief is about building it to be worth that.

EVERY FIGURE BELOW WAS READ FROM THE DEPLOYED WORKER OR THE COMMITTED SOURCE

ON 15 AUGUST 2026. NONE OF THEM IS REMEMBERED.

I · Where it stands

The reciter works and is honest about what it is. Its own header states the privacy
position plainly: audio is NEVER captured or stored — recognition
processes it live and we send only a coverage RATIO. That was the right call and
it stays.

Piece State Where 

The reciter shipped, two paths amenti-readaloud.js 

Coverage floor 0.80 client and Worker agree 

Curve 20 · 12 · 8 · 5 · 3 per topic, resets daily 

Verification none — the client sends the number /readaloud/complete 

Honour mode a timer that pays in full startHonor() 

Artefact nothing survives the session — 

The fleet already flagged half of this

fleet-semantics.js, on the mint's watch: ONE SOFT SPOT:
/readaloud/complete TRUSTS the client's coverage number — bounded by a decaying
curve and a verified identity, so low severity. And then the line that matters:
BUT A HAND-READING IS NOT A PROBE. This is UNPROVEN until an instrument
exists.

The half it did not flag is worse. startHonor() exists because
Safari and Firefox have no SpeechRecognition, and it pays the full
reward on a timer paced at roughly 360ms a word. No microphone is required. On those
browsers, reading aloud is waiting aloud — and that is not an exploit anybody
has to discover, it is the default behaviour on most of the phones in the world.

II · What the faucets actually pay

Measured against the deployed constants and the fifty-nine live quizzes.

One quiz, flawless
280 ◈

The whole library
16,520 ◈

Read-aloud, one topic/day
48 ◈

All 59 topics, one day
2,832 ◈

In circulation now
512 ◈

Three readings follow from that table and each one is a decision, not a tuning knob.

The quiz faucet is already correct — and correct by accident

The unique index emerald_ledger_once means a
question pays once per seeker, ever. Lifetime quiz minting is therefore bounded
at roughly 16,520 ◈ and cannot be farmed. That index was written for correctness rather
than for economics, and it happens to encode the Book's own logic: you cannot pass
the same gate twice. Leave it exactly as it is.

Read-aloud is the only unbounded faucet, and it out-pays thinking

The curve resets daily, per topic. Fifty-nine topics is 2,832 ◈ a day for one
determined person — on the order of a million a year. Meanwhile a complete quiz, six
questions answered and two arguments filed for the docket, pays 280.

Reading aloud currently pays roughly ten times better than thinking, every single
day. That does not promote reading aloud. It makes everything else pointless, and
it is the one number in the economy that cannot survive contact with a thousand
users.

The pool does not scale, and it scales backwards

POOL_SIZE is 500 ◈ a week regardless of how many people
exist. One seeker takes 500. A thousand seekers split 500. The reward for arguing
therefore collapses precisely as the hall fills — and the 500 is minted from nothing,
so it is 26,000 ◈ of pure annual issuance whether or not anyone argued.

III · Four decisions

1 · The pool is the stake, not a subsidy

A vote burns 5 ◈. Settlement mints 500 ◈. Those are two unrelated flows, and joining
them closes the loop: the pool is what the hall actually staked, redistributed.

Fixed pool Parimutuel 

Scales with users no automatically 

Inflationary 26,000 ◈/yr zero — nothing new is minted 

A quiet week pays 500 anyway pays nothing, correctly 

The myth the house funds the argument Ma'at weighs what was put on the scales 

Keep POOL_SIZE as a floor for thin weeks if a floor is
wanted. But a pool conjured from nothing is not a weighing. On 14 August the court sealed
its first case on zero votes and distributed nothing — and that was the system behaving
correctly. The parimutuel makes that the rule rather than the accident.

2 · Honour mode acknowledges. It does not mint.

Two options and no third. Either those browsers get real capture via
MediaRecorder — which works everywhere
SpeechRecognition does not — or honour mode records the read and
pays nothing. A timer must never be a faucet.

3 · Cap the day, and invert the curve

Keep the daily reset. Repetition is the point — it is practice, and practice is what a
reading site is for. But two changes:

Now Proposed Why 

Daily ceiling none ~60 ◈ across all topics 
three careful passages earn what fifty-nine ground ones do 

Curve 20 · 12 · 8 · 5 · 3 rising, not falling 
the fifth read of one passage is the one that is improving.
A falling curve pays for showing up; a rising one pays for fluency. 

4 · Verify without storing

Keeping every reading is expensive and most readings will not be good. So:
cache only, and the seeker keeps their own. The audio need not be stored to be
checked — it needs to reach the Worker once. Score it there, discard the audio, keep the
number. That closes the soft spot the fleet already named, and it costs one request
rather than one object per read, forever.

IV · The certificate

This is the piece that changes what the emerald is for, and it is the reason
the rest of this brief is worth doing.

It is the Negative Confession, in reverse

Spell 125 is forty-two declarations of what was not done. The
certificate is the affirmative form, and it is declared by the record rather than by
the seeker: I read this passage aloud. I cleared eighty-four per cent. I answered
six of six on the Rubicon. I staked twelve emeralds on arguments that were not my
own. Same structure, opposite sign — and both are statements of the conditions
for legitimate passage.

The audio is the proof. The metadata is the claim. A file that says
some audio is a voice memo. A file that says this reading, of this passage,
by this seeker, on this date, at this coverage is checkable, and it is the thing a
person actually keeps.

Carries From 

the reading the session cache — never stored server-side 

passage, work, room, figure library/<key>.json 

coverage, date, duration /readaloud/complete 

quiz standing, hearts and quills /quiz/progress 

ledger balance emerald_balance 

votes cast — the seeker's own argument_votes 

Two things to get right before the shape is fixed

Sign it. The mint already holds the primitive —
makeSession and readSession HMAC with the
service key. The same primitive signs a certificate, and a seeker's claim then verifies
without the Worker surrendering anything private. An unsigned certificate is a text
file anybody can write.

Do not let it de-anonymise the hall. /hall publishes arguments and vote counts
with no author attached, deliberately. A seeker publishing their own
participation is theirs to publish — but the format must not make it easy to correlate
a certificate back to the anonymous rows. Decide the field list with that in mind, not
afterwards.

And the format itself: audio plus a JSON sidecar is honest and ugly. Audio plus a
rendered card — terminal standard, the figure's plate, the numbers set in Share
Tech Mono — is the thing somebody shares. The plates, the fonts and the whole visual
language already exist.

V · What this does to the economics

It makes them almost incidental, which is the point.

Three rewards, three instruments — instead of one doing all three badly

The record acknowledges. Every read that clears coverage writes a row —
passages read, days running, texts finished aloud. Uncapped, unfarmable, and worth more
to a serious reader than the currency.

The emerald pays for what is scarce. Not the read — the record already
acknowledged that. The first complete read of a text, a return after a gap, coverage
well above the floor. Small numbers that stay meaningful at ten thousand users.

The certificate is the reason. Your own voice, on a dead person's sentence,
with the record attached and signed. It costs nothing to issue and it is worth more
than any payout.

THE EMERALD BECOMES A THANK-YOU RATHER THAN A WAGE.

THAT IS THE VERSION THAT SURVIVES TEN THOUSAND USERS.

VI · Order of work

# Step Blocks 

1 Honour mode stops minting. One condition. It is
the largest hole and the cheapest patch. everything downstream 

2 Daily ceiling across all topics. One constant
and one query. the 2,832/day sweep 

3 Server-side scoring. Audio reaches the Worker
once, is scored, is discarded. Closes the soft spot the fleet
named. the certificate's credibility 

4 The record table. One row per cleared read.
Same shape as bell_log — an attendance register. streaks, the certificate 

5 The certificate. Signed, rendered, downloadable.
The reading is cache-only throughout. — 

6 The parimutuel pool. Independent of the above and
can be done any time. 26,000 ◈/yr of issuance 

VII · Open, and not answered here

· Is 512 a cap or a reading? It is 2⁹, which looks deliberate — but nothing in
the code enforces it, and one seeker sweeping the library mints thirty-two times that.
If it is a cap, the whole issuance schedule is two orders of magnitude out. If it is
a measurement, then what needs designing is the rate. Nothing else in this
brief can be finally costed until that is settled.

· Does a great reading get offered a home? Cache-only by default — but a read
that clears a high bar could be invited into the reading room. One voice per
passage, chosen. Rare enough to cost nothing, and it turns reading aloud into
something that can be won rather than ground.

· Coverage is a weak instrument. 0.80 of the words, in order, with a
three-word look-ahead. It measures neither pace, nor pauses, nor whether the reading
was any good. It is the right floor for a read happened. It is not a measure
of a reading worth keeping, and step 5 will want one.

Amenti.live · Ingram Manor LLC

Engineering Brief · The Living Voice · Rev. A · 15 August 2026

Constants read from the deployed mint · quiz count read from /quiz/topics

  BRIEF 36 OF 50  ·  THE SALVAGE · AMENTI TECHNICAL BRIEF

  file     BRIEF-THE-SALVAGE.html
  written  2026-08-22

THE SALVAGE · Amenti Technical Brief
· 

Technical Brief · Ingram Manor LLC

22 August 2026 · recovering a session from mid-July

A session nobody could open

TheSalvage

Seven hundred and sixty-four turns, out of a stuck tab

A browser tab had been sitting open for weeks holding a conversation too
long to answer. It would accept no new message and it could not be exported.
Whatever was in it was legible and unreachable, which is a state this project
has met before in other forms.

A console script walked it and pulled out 764 turns · 41,351 words.
This is what was in there, and what it cost to find out.

I · Why a scraper and not a copy

The obvious approach is select-all and paste, and it does not work — for a
reason that is worth stating because it is the same reason everything else in
this fleet is built the way it is.

A long thread is virtualised. The browser keeps the visible turns in
the document and discards the rest as you scroll. Select-all gets you whatever
happened to be mounted at that instant, silently, with no indication
that the middle is absent.

A COPY THAT LOOKS COMPLETE AND IS NOT

IS THE FAULT THIS WHOLE PROJECT IS ABOUT.

So the script scrolls the container from the top in overlapping steps —
forty per cent overlap, because a clean page-height step can unmount a turn
before it is read and mount the next one after, and the gap never appears in
the output. It collects as it goes, reports its count every eight passes, and
says so aloud when the count stops growing while there is page left.

And the file it writes carries a header that will not let a reader forget
what it is:

READ THIS BEFORE TRUSTING IT: this was scraped from a
rendered page, not exported. The count above is what was FOUND, not what
EXISTS. If the conversation feels like it is missing a middle, it
is.

Two faults in the instrument, recorded

Role detection failed

Every turn came out labelled the same. The selector looked for
markup this interface does not use, so the transcript reads as one voice
talking to itself for forty thousand words. The content survived; the
attribution did not.

It captured containers as well as contents

764 turns deduplicated to 48 unique blocks — the walker
collected parent elements and their children separately, so a long turn
appears once whole and again in pieces. Harmless to read, and it means the
turn count in the header is roughly sixteen times the truth.

Which is its own small lesson: the instrument reported its count
honestly and the count still meant something other than what it looked
like.

II · What was in it

The session was the one that read the nine briefs — Black Hole first,
as the handoff instructed — and then went looking. It is the most productive
session in the recovered record and it found four things.

1 · The most consequential code in the fleet was not under version control

This is the finding that reshaped everything after it.

The ship The Worker 

lives in git a text box in a browser tab 

history every commit none 

diff yes none 

rollback yes none 

the Glass Gate sees it yes no 

a scan can catch a change yes no 

And what that file contained: /speak, the cache key, the R2 put,
the model string, publishWeek(), scheduled(), the
seal, the mint, and the TOPICS map that is the only place a defendant's name
exists.

EVERY EXPENSIVE ACCIDENT IN THIS SYSTEM

LIVED IN THE ONE FILE NOBODY COULD DIFF.

The 13 July handoff had told the next session to check
wrangler.toml — it may be a three-line fix. There was no
wrangler.toml. The handoff sent the next session to a file that
does not exist, and the next session found that out by looking rather than
by trusting.

The Workers mirror — six Workers pulled from the Cloudflare API into a
repository every six hours — exists because of this session.

2 · A chunk profile is a cache namespace

The sharpest technical finding, and it is one line:

The key is sha256(model + voice + style + text). The
text is one measure — and the measure boundaries are set by the profile. So: a
chunk profile is a cache namespace.

recital: 320 and gabriel: 700 split the same
passage into different measures, which hash to different keys, which are
different objects in R2. Two profiles quietly maintaining two archives of the
same audio.

And the consequence for testing, which is the part that would have been
missed: a canonical sentence proves the archive for the profile it was
chunked under, and no other. Pick the sentence before ruling on the
profile and the ward has a hole in it exactly the size of whichever cylinder
was not fired.

That is why the archive watch fires six wires and not one.

3 · Instrument the change, don't instrument the wreck

Asked whether to unify the forked archive first or build the ward first, the
session argued for the ward — and the reasoning generalises past this system:

Do not unify first. Build the ward across both namespaces, exactly
as they stand. Unifying the archive is a cache-key change — the single most
expensive class of change in this system. Doing it before the tripwire
exists is doing it blind, which is how this fleet got here.

With the ward in place the migration proves itself: flip the surface from one
profile to the other, and the wires on the abandoned profile go dark on
purpose while the rest stay green. A planned darkness is a reading. An
unplanned one is a fault. You cannot tell them apart without the instrument in
place first.

4 · council cannot be banished from the ship

A naming census found the string counsel doing two jobs at once
in the DOM of Page1 — the Board Room's page identity and the dyad's
mode value — at four separate places. A clean-up was proposed: one name changes,
and council is banished from the ship.

Then the read that killed it.

The catch

Library text is read aloud through /speak. It is
the hashed text. Edit one word of gallic-war-7.md and every
measure of that document orphans in R2 — and you have altered a
public-domain primary source, which is the moat the prospectus is built
on.

council cannot be banished from the ship. It is history's word before it
was ours.

A repo-wide find-and-replace would not have broken the build. It would have
quietly edited Caesar.

The proposed rule — council does not appear in the ship — would have
been permanently and correctly RED, and the session caught that before writing
it. Rules about names have to be scoped to our own identifiers, because
the library is full of other people's.

III · The tenth brief, and a habit that shipped

The session also read a brief written by the session before it — the one that
had not read the nine — and its verdict is the most useful sentence in
the salvage:

It is the tenth brief, and it has to be read against the other
nine rather than alongside them, because it was written by the session that
hadn't read them. The error is not one error. It is a habit, and this time it
shipped.

The specific case: that brief called AMENTI_VOICE a stowaway —
a fourth speech engine, inline in Page1, on no manifest, listed nowhere
— and the ninth brief had it in a table, deliberately, with a reason. The
Terminal stays off the throttle on purpose because its replies are short live
chat.

A finding reported as a discovery when it was already documented is not a
small error. It is a reader who did not read, and the output is
indistinguishable from genuine work until somebody checks.

IV · The argument against the whole enterprise

And then, unprompted, the thing that makes this salvage worth the trouble.
Asked directly, the session argued the other side:

Honestly? Half of it. Let me argue the other side properly, because
you asked, and because nobody else in this conversation will.

You have one user. Zero endorsements. Thirteen cases and no arguments. A
newsletter with six unpublished Sundays. The Board Room's cross-talk problem is
a problem no stranger has ever encountered, because no stranger has ever been
in the room.

Your own prospectus says it plainly: cost is solved, and audience is where the
work and the risk live. Building a continuous monitoring pane for surfaces
nobody uses is instrumenting an empty room.

That is on the record and it should stay on the record. It is the most useful
paragraph in forty thousand words, and it is the one thing a brief would never
have preserved — because a brief is written by somebody who has already decided
to carry on.

V · What this was worth

The honest accounting, since the question was asked out loud before the
script finished running: is it worth it, or are we sifting our own
chaff?

What came out Was it already recorded? 

The Worker is not in version control No. This session found it, and the mirror exists because of it. 

A chunk profile is a cache namespace Partly — the Conversation Matrix has the key. The namespace consequence was worked out here. 

Instrument the change, not the wreck No. Not in any brief. 

council belongs to history, not to us No. And it would have quietly edited Caesar. 

The tenth-brief verdict No. A brief cannot review the brief that follows it. 

The argument against shipping No. And nothing else in the record makes it. 

Five of six were not written down anywhere.

A BRIEF TELLS YOU WHAT WAS DECIDED.

A TRANSCRIPT SHOWS THE THREE THINGS BELIEVED FIRST,

AND HOW LONG EACH WAS BELIEVED FOR.

The briefs remain the better record for almost everything — thirty-nine
accounts, eighty thousand words, each one written by somebody deciding what
mattered that day. But there is one class of material a brief cannot hold: the
moment before somebody understood. The wrong theory, the confident bad fix,
the argument nobody won.

The Black Hole is made entirely of that, and it is the best document in the
repository.

VI · What to do about the rest

There are other tabs. Two sessions were named as June — Ascension's raw
material — and both ended at a token limit rather than at a conclusion.

If they are salvaged, the fixes worth making to the instrument first:

roles find the selector this interface actually uses, or the transcript is one voice for forty thousand words 

nesting drop any block wholly contained in another before writing the file, or the count is sixteen times the truth 

speed full-viewport steps at 120ms rather than 60% at 260ms — this run took the better part of an hour 

stall warning three quiet passes is normal on a thread this size. It cried wolf twice in the first fifth. 

And the standing note, which applies to the salvage and to everything else
recovered this week: a scrape is evidence of what was found. It is not
evidence of what was there. The header says so, and the header should stay.

Amenti · Ingram Manor LLC

A shop-floor account · 22 August 2026

Recovering a session from mid-July · 764 turns · 48 unique


==============================================================================
  BRIEF 37 OF 50  ·  THE WATER BETWEEN · AMENTI TECHNICAL BRIEF

  written  2026-08-20

THE WATER BETWEEN · Amenti Technical Brief
· 

Technical Brief · Ingram Manor LLC

17 – 20 August 2026

A shop-floor account

TheWater Between

Every fault we found lived between two registers

This is an account of one long session. It is not a manual and it will not
be updated — it is correct about the days it describes and makes no claim
about today.

The session began with a font that would not load and ended with six
repositories bundled, verified and held on a different provider. In between it
laid two ghosts, built four instruments that had never existed, found a
repository nobody remembered, read seven briefs nothing had ever read, and
wrote the first two chapters of a book.

The thread running through all of it was not planned. It emerged, and
it is the reason this brief has the title it does.

EVERY FAULT WE FOUND LIVED IN THE WATER BETWEEN TWO REGISTERS.

NOT ONE WAS AN ERROR INSIDE A REGISTER.

I · The two ghosts

A moment mistaken for a man

gw-winter sat in the plate register holding a card and a thumb
and missing its terminal. Counted. Reported. Incomplete. A figure named
gw who had never been onboarded and whom no roster row could reach.

He was George Washington at Valley Forge. Plates resolve by convention —
{key}-{variant} — so a scene dropped into img/
parses as a figure whose name is a place. The checker was not wrong. The
grammar had no room for the sentence.

A FIGURE KEY CAN BE WRONG — THE ROSTER SAYS WHAT RIGHT IS.

A SCENE TAG COULD NOT BE WRONG, BECAUSE NOTHING SAID WHAT RIGHT WAS.

AUTHORITY IS WHAT MAKES ERROR POSSIBLE.

A man split into two ghosts

Caesar was the other kind. caesar held the thumb, the terminal
and the reading room — the Gallic War in eight books, the Civil War in three.
julius-caesar held a card and nothing else. One man, two keys,
both halves reported incomplete for months, and nothing in the fleet could
see they were the same person.

A rename begun and abandoned after a single file. And it carried a fuse
nobody had noticed: Augustus Caesar is also on the roster. The key did
not merely look untidy — two souls could claim it.

Repairing it took the whole apparatus: a rekey workflow that
carries the manifest records with the files, a reroom workflow that
moves a catalog and its texts together, and a correction to both when each
reintroduced the fault it was written to prevent. Julius is whole. Augustus
was then onboarded onto ground that had been deliberately cleared — the
first soul in the project's history to arrive that way.

II · The keyring

The ghosts were symptoms. The disease was that plates, rooms, scenes and
the proxy all point at each other by key, and nothing ever declared what a key
is.

Not one convention. Four:

Roster name Key Rule 

Abraham Lincoln lincoln the forename dropped 

Cleopatra VII cleopatra the epithet dropped 

Albert Einstein einstein-albert word order · accepted 16 Aug 

George Washington george-washington full — two Washingtons 

Every one was reasonable in the moment. Together they are a habit,
and a habit cannot be checked. Which is why the same argument came round
every few weeks: every checker anyone wrote was a guess at the habit, every
guess was approximately right, and approximately right is how
wd-gann hid behind one character.

tools/keyring.js walks the roster and the plates and names four
states. The third had never existed anywhere:

RESOLVED exactly one soul answers 

LOADED one soul today; another name on the roster would also answer 

AMBIGUOUS more than one soul answers right now 

ORPHAN no soul answers at all 

A KEY IS NOT WRONG UNTIL A SECOND SOUL ANSWERS TO IT.

BY THEN IT IS EXPENSIVE.

First run: 51 resolved, 2 ambiguous, 1 orphan. Final run: 53 resolved,
zero loaded, zero ambiguous, zero orphan.

III · Nothing watched the panes

On 17 August index.html — the Harbor, the way in — was replaced
by a commit reading "Update fmt.Println message from 'Hello' to
'Goodbye'". Go. A hello-world. The entrance to the fleet was an unrelated
file for twelve hours.

No instrument noticed, in a fleet built entirely out of instruments. It was
found by clicking a link.

Count what had a register: plates, keys, wiring, watches, Workers, the
specification. Nothing walked the panes. The surface a reader arrives on
first was the one surface with nothing pointed at it.

probes/probe-panes.mjs now reads every pane in
fleet-nav.js — on disk and in the world, because those answer
different questions. Correct on disk and 404 in the world is a deploy failure.
Serving but structurally wrong is the Harbor case. Three different repairs.

Found by testing the probe before shipping it

With no network, every pane came back UNREACHABLE and the disk
findings were masked behind it. Fifteen simultaneous deploy failures is one
problem, not fifteen. It now detects that nothing served, sets the fetch
half aside, and reports GOOD ON DISK — reachability unknown, which is a
different answer from bad.

Its first live run found a fault introduced during the Harbor's own recovery:
the restored file mounted no nav. A recovery restores what was there, not what
should have been.

IV · The specification, audited by the ship

Twenty-one requirements extracted from the Book of the Dead and the briefs,
stated so a machine can check them, walked against the running system every six
hours.

The order matters more than the count, and it is the reverse of what anyone
would expect. Before the specification was allowed to judge the ship, four
probes were sent at the specification. Three of its entries were wrong.

The rule as drafted What the ship said 

every KV key must carry a TTL 22 of 24 writes are durable storage. The rule would have failed correct code. 

a probe may not POST 15 POSTs in the proxy are the generator. A rule wide enough to condemn the engine teaches its reader to ignore it. 

the roster may not carry a rank rank is build order. An ordering, not a station. 

— missing entirely — nothing declared what a key is. Three incidents traced to it. 

THE SPECIFICATION WAS AUDITED BY THE SHIP

BEFORE THE SHIP WAS AUDITED BY THE SPECIFICATION.

Four answers, and two of them are the honest ones

CONFIRMED and CONTRADICTED are obvious. UNPROVEN means nothing
measures this — not a gap in the ship, a limit of what a probe can know. 
UNREACHABLE means checkable in principle and out of reach from here.

Nothing can measure whether a summons leaned. A probe that guessed would be
inventing, which is the exact fault the specification exists to catch. Four
spells print UNPROVEN forever, with the reason, as loudly as a pass.

And the probe flagged itself

The spend check was clean. Then a comment was added explaining what the check
catches — and the comment wrote its own examples out literally, so the detector
matched them. A comment added to make an instrument honest made it report a
violation that did not exist.

Comments are now stripped before any source scan. And where a check is weaker
than the spell it enforces, the reading says so in the CONFIRMED itself:
the spend check is a pattern over source text, so a method held in a variable
would pass it. That limit is printed rather than assumed.

A CONFIRMED THAT OVERSTATES ITSELF
IS A GREEN LAMP WITH NOTHING BEHIND IT.

V · The chart, and the sixth island

By this point the pattern was legible. gw-winter was a scene the
plate register counted as a figure. Julius was one man across two keys no single
register could see. The Harbor was replaced and nothing watched panes.

Not one was an error inside a register. Every one of them was reporting
truthfully about its own island.

Six registers existed, each perfect about its own territory, and nothing
above them. Five repositories that did not know the others existed — and the
shape of the system existed only in one person's head, which is the one place it
was not written down.

tools/chart.js walks every repository and records existence and
ownership: every file, and which register speaks for it. Not contents —
six registers already do that better, and a second opinion is a second truth.

UNCLAIMED is the finding. A file aboard that no register names is not
necessarily wrong, but nobody is watching it, and this week established what
that costs.

Gameroom0.0 · found, not remembered

A sixth repository. 205 commits, a chess-referee Durable Object
live in Cloudflare, and it was on no list until somebody happened to look. The
ark's own comment warns about exactly this failure — and it had already
happened before the first run.

VI · The ark

Five repositories on one account share one failure domain. A suspended
account, a compromised token, a mistaken deletion — none of those are helped by
a second copy on the same platform.

And a zip is the working tree at one moment. On 17 August a zip taken that
afternoon would have preserved the hello-world and lost the Harbor, and
nothing would have said so.

So: git bundle — one file per repository holding every branch,
every tag, the entire history. Restore is git clone
<repo>.bundle. Shipped to R2, which is a different provider, weekly
and unattended.

A BACKUP THAT WAS NEVER READ BACK IS NOT A BACKUP.

IT IS A FILE WITH A HOPEFUL NAME.

Every bundle is opened and verified before it is called good. The destination
is listed afterwards and the count compared, because an upload returning 200
is not a file that arrived. One missing repository fails the run: a partial
ark that passes is how you discover, on the worst day, that the repository you
needed was the one that quietly stopped being included.

Verification found a bug in the verifier. git bundle verify
must run from inside a repository; the first cut called it from the runner's
working directory and would have failed on all six.

Amenti.live 52M verified
Fleet-Documents 600K verified
Amenti-Technical-Briefs 7.7M verified
Amenti-Workers 208K verified
Admin 60K verified
Gameroom0.0 1.6M verified

6 bundles at s3://amenti-ark/2026-08-19
the ark is off-provider

And the credential was narrowed

An R2 token named chess-referee build token had been sitting since
May with Admin Read & Write on all buckets — able to delete AUDIO,
SCENES, and now the ark. It was made for one deploy. Nothing automated used it;
the repository has no workflows and the machine cannot run Node.

Deleted, along with an inactive agent token. One R2 credential now exists:
ark, Object Read & Write, scoped to one bucket.

VII · The briefs nothing could read

The hub's Engineering tab lists eight briefs. The repository holds
forty-one — and the tab does not say it is showing eight.

The first source index was built from that tab and was therefore missing
thirty-three documents, including the Speech Doctrine and the Sacred Frames:
the two capabilities the captain names as the ones that make the site what it
is.

Two instruments came out of it.

SOURCES.json — sixty-four sources with fetchable paths, grouped
by subject, with the traps written down. It is the first register of the
things the machine does not read. Every other file aboard has a consumer;
the briefs had none, which is why a list showing eight of forty-one could sit
there indefinitely.

tools/bundle-briefs.js — every brief concatenated into one plain
text file, with a table of contents and the date each was first committed. 39
briefs · 80,107 words · 479 KB. Not one undated.

The dates tell their own story. Nineteen briefs were written on 18 July.
Something happened that Thursday.

The bundler bundled the wrong repository

Uploaded to Amenti.live by mistake, it did exactly what it was
told: walked the repository it was in and bundled nine surfaces —
Page1, Page2, the reader, the court — as though they were accounts of the
building. It reported cleanly and went green. A tool cannot know which
repository it should be in; it can know which one it is in, and refuse. It
now does.

VIII · What the briefs said about the voice

Seven audio briefs were read — the first time anything had read them. They
line up into one architecture.

The engine rests on a single separation, and everything else follows from
it. Cognition and articulation are different faculties and they are never
braided into one pipeline.

A MIND. A VOICE. ONE NERVOUS SYSTEM.
DO NOT BRAID THEM.

The flood. A whole document handed to the synthesizer in one call dies
at 524 every time. No human orator speaks a thousand words without pausing
for air. The cure was metering, not a bigger cylinder.

The cache key is sha256(TTS_MODEL + voice + STYLE + TEXT),
and because TEXT is a single measure, the chunk boundaries ARE the cache
namespace. Move one byte of the model string, the style or the chunker and
every clip in the archive orphans — silently — and must be re-rendered at
cost.

The Worker lives in Cloudflare, outside version control. No diff, no history,
no rollback. So it is not read:

THE WORKER CANNOT BE READ. SO IT IS ASKED.

SIX HITS = THE KEY HELD. ONE MISS = THE ARCHIVE FORKED.

And the finding that justifies the pane

Page1's Terminal is an IIFE. It executes at parse time, line 5989, and
asks for window.Amenti.chat. The file that registers it loads at
line 6925 — 936 lines too late. The guard evaluated false,
termChat became null, and every call site silently took the inline
fallback.

The handover said the opposite, in capitals: amenti-chat.js is the PRIMARY
PATH — always, normally. Session after session improved an engine sitting on
the dock, beautifully maintained, connected to nothing. Fourteen harnesses
reported green across 316 assertions, each one creating its own object and
testing that.

BEING LOADED IS NOT BEING USED.

That is measurable from source — load order against use order — and it is what
the voice pane is for. It is not built. It is outlined, with every dependency
named, and it is blocked on nine files nobody has read.

IX · What shipped

Instrument Repository What it reads · writes 

tools/keyring.js Amenti.live roster + plates → KEYS.json 

tools/chart.js Amenti.live every repo → CHART.json. Existence and ownership. 

probes/probe-spells.mjs Amenti.live the spec → spell-conformance.json 

probes/probe-panes.mjs Fleet-Documents every pane, disk and world → PANES.json 

tools/bundle-briefs.js Briefs 39 briefs → BRIEFS.txt 

ark.yml Amenti.live 6 repos → R2, and ARK.json through the gate 

rekey.yml · reroom.yml Amenti.live a figure's key, and a room, moved whole 

spells.yml · panes.yml · bundle.yml both the schedules that run the above 

Surfaces

keyring.html the 15th pane. Every key and what it reaches. 

watch.html gained THE ARK — green only when the copy is off-provider, red when stale. 

workers.html gained the topology: how flat files and stateless compute make a page. 

ocean.html four faults fixed. UNPROVEN no longer renders as CONFIRMED. 

index.html the Harbor, recovered from git, nav restored. 

read.html · BOOK.json the book reader. Five bands, and THE UNWRITTEN PAGE. 

The book

book/00-the-beach.md — the prologue, in the captain's own prose.
book/02-the-specification.md — the first chapter through the full
apparatus. And ASCENSION was found: the volume that precedes Book Zero,
covering June, when the system learned to think and speak.

X · The readings, at the close

keys 53 resolved · 0 loaded · 0 ambiguous · 0 orphan
plates 51 keys · 51 cards · 51 thumbs · 50 terminals
spells 8 confirmed · 0 contradicted · 4 unproven · 8 unreachable
panes 14 · none stranding · none missing · 8 unstyled
ark 6 repositories · verified · off-provider
briefs 39 · 80,107 words · all dated
roster 1,011 souls

THE SHIP IS WELL INVENTORIED.

UNTIL THIS WEEK, THE FLEET WAS NOT.

XI · What this cost, and what it is worth

Almost nothing found this week was broken. The plate register was correct. The
key checkers were correct. The watches reported honestly. The Harbor's
replacement was a clean commit.

What was missing was the instrument aimed at the space between them —
and that space is where every fault had been living, undisturbed, for weeks.

The transferable version, for whoever inherits this:

A register that is perfect about its own territory will report truthfully and
completely while a fault sits one inch outside it. Six such registers are not
six times safer than one. They are six islands, and the water between them
is unwatched by construction.

The instruments built this week — the keyring, the chart, the pane watch, the
source index — are all the same instrument pointed at different water. That was
not the plan. It is what the week turned out to be about.

Amenti · Ingram Manor LLC

A shop-floor account · 17–20 August 2026

Correct about the days it describes · no claim about today


==============================================================================
  BRIEF 38 OF 50  ·  FRAUD OR FICTION · AMENTI TECHNICAL BRIEF

  written  2026-09-03

Fraud or Fiction · Amenti Technical Brief

AMENTI · TECHNICAL BRIEF · 2 SEPTEMBER 2026

Fraud or Fiction

The first honest cohort added to the roster from an external
source — and the seventeen fictional presidents the source tried to
smuggle in.

The source is a draft too.

— the lesson, learned in the first thirty seconds

The occasion

Expanding the roster without becoming the source.

The roster is a closed list — the hall knows only the souls in
names.csv, and its wall is hard. To grow it (THE
STANDING SLIP #52) the honest way is to draw from an
external, checkable source, never from the model's memory
(#54): the interface is the tool, not the
source. This brief records the first cohort done that way — the
US presidents — as the worked proof of the pattern.

It was chosen because it is small, modern, and unambiguous: a set where a
wrong entry is obvious. Which turned out to matter, because the source handed
us wrong entries immediately.

The first fetch

Sixty-three &ldquo;presidents,&rdquo; and seventeen who never lived.

The naive query — everyone Wikidata records as holding the office
of President of the United States — returned 63
names. The property is polluted with fictional office-holders from film,
television and comics, undated and indistinguishable, at the top of the list.
A fetch-and-commit would have put every one of these on the roster:

flagged out — 17 fictional office-holders

Mr. Garrison
Gonzo (Mechanical)
Thor
Doom 2099
Zartan
Prez
General Joseph Colton
Glen Allen Walken
President Winthrop
Robert L. Booth
Olivia Marsdin
Quentin Trembley
Thomas Kirkman
Ed Kealty
Phil Baker
Roger Durling
The President

This is the whole argument for the architecture, delivered in one screen.
The source is not the truth; it is a draft that must itself be
verified. An external reference is safer than the model's memory
— but only because it can be checked, not because it is clean. Trusting
it blind is the same failure as trusting the model blind, wearing a more
respectable coat.

The pattern

Six steps, none skippable.

1
Fetch from a real source.
A SPARQL query to Wikidata, not a recitation from memory. The model
navigates to the source; it does not become it.

2
Filter the fiction.
Constrain to actual humans ?p wdt:P31 wd:Q5 with a real
birth date. The 17 fictional holders fall away;
45 real ones remain.

3
Complete the record.
Pull both dates. Deaths were missing on the first pass; a second query
recovered them — and caught a fresh fact memory might have missed:
Carter, died 2024.

4
Cross-check the roster.
Seven presidents were already aboard. Zero date conflicts
with the existing roster — source and ship agree on every overlap,
which is its own signal both are sound.

5
Let the guard fire.
probe-roster over the expanded list: 1,049
souls, all placeable, zero bad dates, the long-life count
unchanged. The sign-error guard (#46) passed the
whole batch.

6
Record provenance.
Every new row carries its Wikidata entity in the Links column. The row does
not say Lincoln, 1809&ndash;1865; it says that per a citable
source. The model pointed; the source confirmed; the row records both.

What landed

Thirty-eight, dated and sourced.

63
returned by the naive fetch

17
fictional, filtered out

7
already on the roster

38
new, added with provenance

0
bad dates · 0 conflicts

The presidents now take their place in time: Lincoln
already aboard, now with the Roosevelts, the Adamses, Kennedy and Washington's
whole line beside him on the timeline — each a dated bar, a name in
alive beside them, an anchor the mention graph can reach. None with a
room; none needed one. A soul needs no shelf to have a place in
time.

The principle it proves

Fraud, fiction, and the wall between them.

Two ways a false entry reaches a roster. Fraud is the
model inventing a date from memory and presenting it as fact — the
s&eacute;ance. Fiction is an external source carrying
something that was never real — Doom 2099, sitting in
a reputable database beside George Washington. The ship guards against both by
the same discipline, applied at the same stage: verification at the
workbench, before anything lands.

The model's general knowledge is not banned — it is
quarantined to the authoring stage, where every draft is checked. It
navigated to the source, read the fiction for what it was, and filtered it.
That is the honest use of a fallible intelligence: not as the record, but as
the draftsman who finds the record and is never trusted to be it.

Fraud is what the mouth invents. Fiction is what the source repeats.
The wall stops both, and it stands at the workbench, not the front desk.

And the stakes are permanent, which is the
point. The roster is a ratchet — it turns one way, and a fact
once laid as structure is trusted as structure. So a lie laid as stone is
permanent too: fraud or fiction that survives the workbench does not just add a
wrong row, it converts a false belief into apparent architecture, which is
worse, because architecture is not doubted. The six steps are not hygiene.
They are what guarantees the ratchet only turns truth into permanence.
This cohort was the wheel turning once, cleanly — scaffolding becoming
stone, with the fiction read for what it was and left off the wall. THE
STANDING SLIP #55.

AMENTI · INGRAM MANOR LLC · ONE BUILDER, IN THE OPEN, IN PLAIN TEXT

Source: Wikidata SPARQL, 2 Sep 2026 · 38 rows staged to names.csv with provenance

Companion: THE-SKY-AS-A-SOURCE · BRIEF-ASK-AMENTI ·
THE-STANDING-SLIP #52, #54

The source was verified, not trusted. The fiction was read for what it was.

High-Priority Acquisition · Amenti Technical Brief

AMENTI · ACQUISITION BRIEF · 2 SEPTEMBER 2026

High-Priority Acquisition

One hundred and twenty-three authors of antiquity, now on the
roster, whose works survive and are free — each owed a room.

They have a place in time already. What is owed them is a library.

— the distinction this brief exists to draw

What this is

A roster placement is not a room. These earn both.

Tonight’s expansion added many souls as placements — a
dated bar on the timeline, a name among the living, an anchor for the mention
graph. The kings of Rome are placements: legendary, no works, they do not
speak. These 123 are different. Every one is a real author
whose writing survives in the public domain, cross-referenced tonight to a
Project Gutenberg author record. They are not placements — they are
room-worthy, and this brief names them as the priority
acquisition: the worklist for turning roster rows into reading rooms.

They were found, not remembered. The batch is the intersection of two
structured facts — who wrote (Wikidata) and whose work we
can hold (Gutenberg) — filtered to antiquity, where survival is its
own significance test. There are no minor authors here, because the minor
ancients did not survive to have a record. THE STANDING SLIP
#54, #55.

The haul

From the oldest named author on earth to the edge of the medieval.

123
room-worthy authors, on the roster

2451 BC
the oldest: Ptahhotep

AD 790
the youngest: Li He

100%
with a public-domain source

The span runs from Ptahhotep, the Egyptian vizier whose
maxims are the oldest named authorship in human history, through the Greek and
Chinese and Roman canon, to the Tang poets and Carolingian chroniclers at the
medieval threshold. Homer’s inheritors and Confucius’s, in one
roster, dated and citable.

Priority order

The worklist, oldest and most consequential first.

Room-building is the citation job — each work confirmed against its
Gutenberg edition and graded by the citation policy. Ordered by antiquity,
because the deepest names anchor the most of the timeline. A selection of the
head of the list; the full manifest ships as
ACQUISITION-ANCIENTS.json.

The rule that governs the build

A room is earned, one edition at a time.

· Each is a citation job, not a copy. The Gutenberg link
proves works EXIST; the room requires each work cited to its edition and
graded — the same discipline as every room aboard.

· Some are lost or partial. Varro survives only in part;
much of the tradition is fragments. The room states what survives, honestly,
the way a good room always has.

· These feed the mention graph (#47):
Livy names the kings, Suetonius the Caesars, Josephus his contemporaries. A
cohort that cites each other is where the citable-mention layer pays
off first.

· The order is the priority. Oldest first — the
deepest authors anchor the most of the timeline, and the earliest rooms
enrich the most panels.

The roster gave them a place in time tonight. The library owes them their
voice — found in a real edition, cited to its page, quoted only from
the text.

Alpha Helix Upgrade — Technical Record
· 
· 
· 

AMENTI.LIVE

ALPHA HELIX UPGRADE

A Technical Record of the Double-Helix Text Rail

orientation :: solved :: build: RESOLVED

Enter the technical record

Amenti · Engineering Record

Alpha Helix Upgrade

Diagnosing and resolving the frame-rate collapse of the double-helix view under full population — a measured optimization pass on Page2.

SUBJECT: amenti.live · Page2 (Emerald Tablets / double-helix) · PLATFORM: integrated Apple GPU, Retina (pixelRatio 2) · METHOD: profile-first

Summary

Outcome

With both strands rendered and fully populated (1,002 figure labels + 488 event labels), the double-helix view ran at 4.3 FPS — effectively unusable. Five targeted, measured changes returned it to a usable frame rate and reduced texture memory by 16×, with no loss of visual fidelity at the zoom levels in actual use.

4.3 → ~18

FPS · both strands

≈ 4× faster

5.3GB → 330MB

Texture VRAM

16× reduction

4954 → 1946

Scene objects

−61%

3 → 1

Dominant frame costs

isolated to drawArrays

Every change in this record was made on a working copy, verified against the renderer's own counters and Safari's frame profiler, and validated by re-measurement before the next change. No optimization was applied on intuition alone — a discipline that repeatedly corrected wrong initial assumptions about the bottleneck.

01 · The Problem

A frame-rate collapse under full population

The helix and beta-helix are two interlocking spiral strands. Each figure and historical event is rendered as an individual billboard sprite carrying a baked text label. Singly and lightly populated, the view performs acceptably. With both strands active and full — the heaviest real state — it collapsed:

Metric (initial state) Value Note 

Frame rate 4.3 FPS unusable; 67 of 69 frames over 20 ms 

Worst frame 321 ms visible stalls 

Texture VRAM 5,261 MB on an integrated GPU with no headroom 

Label sprites 1,490 1,002 figures + 488 events 

Per-name texture 2048×256 ≈ 2 MB each, for one line of text 

Draw calls / frame 669 sprites cannot batch 

The root constraint: this is an integrated Apple GPU with unified memory. 5.3 GB of texture data for text labels left no room — the GPU was thrashing texture memory every frame. The starting hypothesis (from an early profiler reading showing 64% "JavaScript" time) was that the animation loop was the bottleneck. That hypothesis was wrong, and proving it wrong was the first job.

02 · Method

Measure, don't guess

Rather than apply plausible fixes, the pass began by instrumenting the live scene. Two read-only console diagnostics were written to extract ground truth the standard profiler view couldn't isolate: a census of sprites, visibility, animation-state, and texture footprint; a microbenchmark of the per-sprite loop; the renderer's draw-call and texture counts; and a CPU-vs-GPU frame-time split.

This discipline paid off by being wrong-proof. Over the course of the pass, the measured data overturned the working hypothesis three separate times:

Assumed bottleneck What the measurement showed 

The animation loop (CPU) Loop cost was 0.12 ms/frame — trivial. The cost was texture memory. 

Transparent-sprite depth sorting Removing 3,000 sprites from the sort barely moved the frame time. Not the cause. 

Per-sprite texture uploads (texImage2D) The dominant upload was the 80,000-vertex helix point-cloud, not the sprite textures. 

A console probe that timed GPU work via a readPixels flush reported "GPU idle, CPU-bound." This was a false reading — Safari's driver deferred the work past the readback. Safari's frame profiler (flame chart) was the only reliable ground truth, and it named the real costs directly. Lesson: trust the profiler over any synthetic probe.

03 · The Interventions

Five changes, each measured

Applied in sequence, each verified before the next. Ordered as performed; impact varied widely — and one change's main value was disproving a hypothesis rather than gaining frames.

1

Texture shrink — the memory fire

Each name was baked into a 2048×256 canvas (≈2 MB) and each event into 2048×512 — roughly 50× the resolution they were ever displayed at. Since names are never zoomed into, the canvases were shrunk to 512×64 and 512×128 respectively (font and coordinates scaled proportionally, aspect ratios preserved so the per-frame scale math stayed valid). A 16× reduction in pixels per texture.

// per-name bake
make2DCanvas(2048, 256) → make2DCanvas(512, 64)
font 'bold 160px' → 'bold 40px' // same fit, 1/16 the pixels

VRAM 5,261 MB → 329 MB · FPS 4.3 → 17.7

2

Lazy companions — parked off-graph

Every figure carried three hidden decoration sprites (a bookmark reticle, a teleport orb, a scan glow-plate) — 3,006 sprites, of which only ~11 are ever active. They were added to the scene graph at build time and traversed every frame though almost always invisible. The change builds them but keeps them detached from the scene graph, attaching a name's companions only while it is bookmarked or teleport-active.

Scene objects 4,954 → 1,946 · draw calls 70 → 26 · FPS ~17 → 17.9

FPS barely moved — which was itself the finding: it proved the transparent-sprite sort was not the bottleneck, redirecting the investigation toward the GPU upload path. A structurally correct change whose chief value was diagnostic.

3

Disable mipmaps on text textures

The flame chart revealed generateMipmap consuming 417 ms — Three.js was building full mipmap chains for all 1,490 text textures, plus the re-uploads that triggers. Text viewed head-on never needs mipmaps (they exist for textures seen shrinking into distance).

tex.generateMipmaps = false;
tex.minFilter = THREE.LinearFilter; // default forces a mip chain

generateMipmap 417 ms → 0 · eliminated from the profile

4

Reduce render resolution (pixelRatio 2 → 1.5)

On a Retina display the renderer drew at pixelRatio 2 — 4× the screen's pixels. With ~1,700 transparent, blended sprites, that overdraw was the bulk of drawArrays. Lowering the cap to 1.5 cut the drawn pixel count ~44% with negligible visible softening on text against black.

setPixelRatio(Math.min(devicePixelRatio, 2)) → Math.min(devicePixelRatio, 1.5)

drawArrays 1,842 ms → 1,644 ms

5

Throttle the helix recompute — the breakthrough

The helix spine is a 40,000-point cloud per strand (80,000 total), recomputed in JavaScript and re-uploaded to the GPU every frame. This — not the sprite textures — was the texImage2D cost dominating the profile. Because the helix rotates slowly (~0.9 rad over 3 s), the recompute and re-upload were throttled to every other frame (30 fps), which is visually identical.

if (frame % 2 === 0) { // recompute + re-upload at 30fps
// …40,000-point spiral, both strands…
helix.geometry.attributes.position.needsUpdate = true;
}

texImage2D 3,142 ms → 0 · removed from the profile entirely

04 · Results

Before and after

FPS · before

4.3

FPS · after

~18

VRAM · before

5,261 MB

VRAM · after

329 MB

Metric Before After Change 

FPS (both strands, full) 4.3 ~18 ≈ 4× 

Worst frame 321 ms ~70 ms −78% 

Texture VRAM 5,261 MB 329 MB −94% 

Scene objects 4,954 1,946 −61% 

Draw calls / frame 669 26 −96% 

generateMipmap 417 ms 0 eliminated 

texImage2D (uploads) dominant 0 eliminated 

Frame-profile evolution

The three dominant GPU costs were removed in turn until a single honest cost remained.

Stage drawArrays texImage2D generateMipmap 

After shrink + lazy companions 2,031 ms 811 ms 417 ms 

After mipmap disable 1,842 ms ~1,200 ms — gone 

After pixelRatio 1.5 1,644 ms dominant* — 

After helix throttle 2,023 ms† — gone — 

*texImage2D became the visible dominant once overdraw fell — exposing the helix point-cloud upload as its true source. †Absolute ms varies with recording length; drawArrays is now the sole dominant cost as a share of the frame (67.6%).

05 · Where the pass stopped, and why

Waste removed; real work remains

After the five changes, the frame is dominated by a single cost — drawArrays (≈68%), the actual rasterization of ~1,700 transparent blended sprites and the helix point clouds. This is no longer waste; it is the genuine cost of drawing a dense, transparent, two-strand helix. Every cheap inefficiency — oversized textures, dead companion sprites, mipmap generation, redundant geometry uploads — has been removed.

Further gains exist but trade visual quality rather than removing inefficiency, which is the correct point to stop a routine optimization pass:

Remaining levers (quality trade-offs)

pixelRatio 1.5 → 1.0 Another ~2× cut to drawArrays fill, at the cost of noticeably softer text on Retina. 

Reduce helix point count 40,000 → fewer points per strand halves draw cost but thins the visible spine. 

Sprite instancing / atlas The structural rewrite to collapse 1,490 sprites into batched geometry. High effort; the deferred "proper" fix. Now feasible (post-shrink, all labels fit one GPU texture). 

Open item — texture disposal

Diagnostics noted resident GL textures creeping upward across strand rebuilds (≈12 over a 4-second window), indicating textures not fully disposed on eject/rebuild. Not a contributor to the frame-rate problem and out of scope for this pass, but the likely cause of any slow degradation over a long session. The existing diagnostic surfaces it.

Appendix

Diagnostic tooling

Three read-only, self-restoring console probes were written for this pass. They mutate nothing and print copy-paste JSON reports. Retained for future use.

Tool Purpose 

amenti-helix-diagnostic.js Sprite/texture census, visibility, animation-state, VRAM and atlas feasibility, loop microbench, live FPS. 

amenti-helix-diagnostic-2.js Post-shrink probe: CPU-vs-GPU split, transparency/scene-graph weight, payoff projections, draw-call counts. 

amenti-frame-profiler.js Wraps the real per-frame work and ranks function cost as text — a console fallback for the flame chart. 

The decisive instrument was not a probe but Safari's built-in Timeline → JavaScript & Events → Call Trees flame chart. The "Self Time" column named the true costs (drawArrays, texImage2D, generateMipmap) that no object-counting probe could see. When a probe and the profiler disagree, the profiler is right.

  BRIEF 41 OF 50  ·  THE CONSTELLATION · AMENTI TECHNICAL BRIEF

The Constellation · Amenti Technical Brief

AMENTI · TECHNICAL BRIEF · 3 SEPTEMBER 2026

The Constellation

The roster is not a list. It is the fixed grid the whole library
is triangulated against — and it compounds.

A name that resolves to nothing falls through, unforced — the way a
signal that hits no satellite is lost, not faked.

— the rule that keeps the bounce honest

The reframe

A resolver, not a register.

A historian’s text is unstructured — a stream of words. Among
them are names: Tiberius, Ptolemy, Vespasian, hundreds more. On their own they
are just strings. Bounced against the roster, each name either resolves
to a known, dated point — or it does not. The ones that resolve
become citable edges. The ones that do not fall through. The roster is the
fixed constellation the corpus is triangulated against; the mention graph is
the set of successful bounces.

Names rise from the text and resolve against the constellation. A beam that reaches a star is a citable edge; one into empty sky falls through; one to the wrong star is caught by disambiguation.

roster point (dated soul)
resolved edge
false resolution, caught

The proof

Sixteen edges, and the six that were caught.

The first harvest — Josephus’s Antiquities, Books
14&ndash;19 — bounced against the roster and resolved
16 confirmed edges: Tiberius, Claudius, Augustus,
Titus, Vespasian, Nero and their contemporaries, each citable to a book. And
it produced 6 false bounces, every one caught:

16
resolved edges, verified

6
false bounces, caught

3
filters: apparatus, name, note

1,501
points in the constellation

· Ptolemy — bounced off the wrong point: the text
means Ptolemy Menneus, a local ruler, not the astronomer.

· Suetonius, Livy — found only in the
translator’s footnotes, not Josephus’s own words.

· Brutus — the room-note excluded it: &ldquo;this is
LUCIUS&hellip; not Marcus.&rdquo; The wrong Brutus.

· Josephus — self-reference; an author is not an
edge to himself.

A substring match is a candidate, never a link. The scan
cannot decide; it can only present every signal a human needs to decide in
seconds — count, date-fit, compound-name warning, room-note exclusion,
first context. A whole book’s edges confirmed in about a minute.

The loop

It compounds. Most corpora decay.

This is the part that matters. The roster and the mention graph feed each
other: a bigger constellation resolves more of the corpus, and the names that
fail to resolve name exactly who is missing. The want-list writes
itself.

The corpus bounces off the roster &#8594; edges resolve, and the misses reveal who to add &#8594; add them from sources &#8594; the constellation grows &#8594; more resolves. The wheel turns one way, toward more.

The consequence

The corpus chooses the next cohort, not fame.

This inverts how roster growth is prioritised. The highest-value souls to
add next are not the most famous — they are the ones the aboard
texts keep naming but cannot resolve. Log the near-misses — a
capitalised name, date-plausible, matching no point — and they are the
next cohort, chosen by the library itself. The mention-graph harvest is also a
want-list generator.

And it stays honest for the same reason everything here does: a bounce
counts only if it resolves to a real dated point. No name is forced to land. A
false resolution — the wrong Ptolemy — is a satellite locking the
wrong beacon, and it is exactly the error the disambiguation exists to catch.

Build the constellation. Bounce the corpus off it. Let the two compound.

AMENTI · INGRAM MANOR LLC · ONE BUILDER, IN THE OPEN, IN PLAIN TEXT

Proof: MENTIONS-josephus.json · 16 edges, 6 caught · harvester: mgraph.mjs

Companion: FRAUD-OR-FICTION · HIGH-PRIORITY-ACQUISITION · THE-SKY-AS-A-SOURCE · SLIP #47 #52 #54 #58 #59

The list became an instrument. History resolves against it.

  BRIEF 42 OF 50  ·  THE SKY AS A SOURCE · AMENTI TECHNICAL BRIEF

The Sky as a Source · Amenti Technical Brief

AMENTI · TECHNICAL BRIEF · 2 SEPTEMBER 2026

The Sky as a Source

A night's work on the timeline, and the claim underneath it:
the planets are a primary source in the fullest sense — the only witness
that is still testifying.

Livy reaches you through a translator. Saturn reaches you through arithmetic
anyone can redo and no one can slant.

— the argument this brief is built on

The claim

The planets are the record; the ephemeris is only its edition.

A figure's room holds what they wrote. A planet's room holds what
it did — its own path, still legible. That makes the sky the
most primary source on the ship. Every human account — Livy, Josephus,
Newton — is filtered through a person, a language, an era. The planets
are unmediated: Caesar's own words reach you through Whiston or Motte; Saturn's
rising reaches you through a calculation that anyone can repeat and no one can
slant.

It answers the citation policy's own test — can a reader reach
the identical thing again? For Livy, they reach the edition. For the
planets, they reach the sky itself: recompute it, or wait and watch it happen.
It is the one source that can never go out of print.

The organizing principle

Not &ldquo;how rare&rdquo; — &ldquo;who could see it.&rdquo;

The sky sorts by a single honest axis: how much of the Earth
witnessed the event. Each mark then claims exactly what it can defend,
no more.

worldwide

Conjunctions, gatherings, Halley. Seen across the
whole night hemisphere at once — China, Cairo and Cahokia the same
night. No location claim needed: it happened is it was
seen. These are the events that genuinely connected all of humanity
at a moment.

latitude-local

Due-east risings over Giza. &ldquo;Rises due
east&rdquo; is a statement about one horizon; from another latitude the
same planet rises at another bearing. Honestly labelled over
Giza, because the Great Pyramid is aligned to true north within
arcminutes — the monument itself defines the line.

track-local

Eclipses — deliberately NOT on the ship.
Visible only along a narrow shadow-path, and the path is the
meaning. &Delta;T — the drift between clock time and the Earth's
rotation — is hours before 1500, so the date
is computable but the place is not. An eclipse with an unreliable location
is worse than useless, because location is its entire content. They belong
in the events register as recorded sightings, where the chronicler
who watched the sky go dark is the source.

What the ship carries

The star chart, in three computed tiers.

All computed from JPL's DE422 ephemeris at the Great
Pyramid, 2894 BC to AD 2026.
Verified against orbital theory — the conjunction count matches
248 to the theory's 248,
which is how a computed source proves itself.

1,342
due-east risings
Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, Neptune

248
great conjunctions
Jupiter meets Saturn, ~20 yr

14
gatherings
all four outer planets in one arc

48
Halley returns
1404 BC to AD 2061

The SKY.csv register is the timeline's precomputed
cargo — fast to draw 1,600 marks at once. It is
primary and recomputable: recompute and get 962
Jupiters instead of 830 and you have hit the aberration
bug the first run hit — the number itself is the proof.

The rhythm

The conjunction is the ship's clock — not its meaning.

The great conjunction returns every ~20 years like
a metronome, but unlike a drawn grid line it is a real event people watched and
marked. Five rings to a century; count 2, 4, 6, 8, 10
and you have walked 200 years. The century is the
downbeat — a brighter full-height rule the eye lands on,
and the conjunction nearest it drawn filled, so the count arrives visibly.

But the rhythm is a clock, not an oracle. Tested against the roster, the
great conjunctions land on exactly as many souls as randomly chosen years
— 33 of 143 against
32 expected. Show a cycle as an anchor; never
let a surface imply it means anything. The rings time the journey;
they do not explain the passengers.

The marks

Each body drawn as itself.

· Planet risings — the astronomical glyph in the
planet's own colour: &#9798; Neptune,
&#9797; Uranus,
&#9796; Saturn,
&#9795; Jupiter. The colours anyone
knows from a photograph, so the row reads before it is explained.

· Conjunctions — a gold ring where the two planets
touch. Not a glyph: the event is a meeting, and the ring says so.

· Gatherings — the rarest mark on the ship, four
coloured orbs stacked, on a full-height beam with the year beside it. You
cannot pass one without seeing it, which is right for something that happens
14 times in five thousand years.

· Halley's Comet — an ice-blue comet with a short
tail on the sky row, drawn from Page2's own curated perihelion list. It
sparkles only at close zoom — a reward for arriving on a
return, not a permanent twinkle. The mark says returns, not
visible from Giza: astronomically true, modest claim.

The instrument

From a chart you read to an instrument you interrogate.

The timeline places a figure in a fixed 200-year window centred on
their midpoint, ordered by death year to agree with Page2's helix.
Both axes scroll — time across, population down — and the vertical
now follows the window, gliding to the band of souls alive in view so
the names populate as you travel through the centuries.

A side panel gives the figure's world: the events of their life, the sky
over Giza in their years, and who was alive beside them — ordered rooms
first, then by overlap, so the most relevant contemporaries rise. Proper names
in terminal blue, so a reader scans for who and where without
reading every line.

Anything clicked broadcasts its span, and everything sharing it lights up.
— the connection gesture

Click a life and everyone who overlapped it glows; click an event and the
people alive when it happened; click a conjunction ring and the generation that
lived under it. Nothing broadcasts a bare year — an
instant broadens to a window, a life stays a span — so a click never
lights nothing. The connection is always overlap, never coincidence of a single
year.

Two holes, avoided

The tempting build that betrays the premise.

Twice in the night the easy path would have hollowed the ship out, and both
times the shortcut was refused.

The fabricated frame

The hall named a work of Josephus that does not exist. The quote guard
proves the words; nothing yet checks the title, book, chapter or
verse that frames them. Recorded as a watch item, not a
build — a naive title-guard would flag a real chapter as a fabrication,
crying wolf on the hall reading closely. It promotes to a build on a second
instance caught on screen.

The planet that spoke

The temptation was to let Jupiter talk — and prose in a planet's
mouth is the model's prose dressed as the planet's: a s&eacute;ance
with better production values. A planet does not speak in prose.
Worse, the first plan baked a static record from SKY.csv
when the ship already runs the ephemeris live — Page2 loads
astronomy-engine as Sovereign.Ephemeris
and computes any classical planet's position for any year in the browser,
agreeing with DE422 to better than 99% across the
whole historical roster. The planet room is that engine queried live,
not a dead copy of it. The law is the feature, not the obstacle.

The night's ledger

What was built, and where it stands.

artifactstate

amenti-timeline.js — the instrumentlive

SKY.csv — three tiers, 1,604 markslive

EVENTS.csv — +48 Halley returnslive

HALL.md — the s&eacute;ance line as epigraphlive

the connection gesture — click to reveallive

the century downbeat — the count landslive

planet rooms — live ephemeris queriesdesigned · #51

the frame guard — title / chapter / versewatch · #50

the science cohort — 30 dated, 4 shelveddesigned · #49

The principle to keep

The oldest primary documents in the library.

The conjunctions, the gatherings, the risings, the returns — these are
not decoration on the timeline. They are read straight from the objects that
wrote them, the one witness that has never stopped testifying. A source so
primary it needs no voice, because its testimony is its path.

Ask me where I was and I will tell you exactly, from my path. Ask me what it
meant and I have no opinion — that is for the ones who watched me.

— the planet's own boundary; no terminal, no voice, a reading room only

AMENTI · INGRAM MANOR LLC · ONE BUILDER, IN THE OPEN, IN PLAIN TEXT

Companion briefs: BRIEF-ASK-AMENTI · BRIEF-WHAT-A-SOURCE-MUST-BE ·
BUILD-THE-TIMELINE · THE-STANDING-SLIP (51 moves)

Written 2 Sep 2026, at the end of a long night. Every number was computed and
checked; the sky was read, not remembered.

The Wall and the Prompt · Amenti
· 
· 
· 

AMENTI · TECHNICAL BRIEF · 1 SEPTEMBER 2026

Twenty thousand
characters.

Everything Ask Amenti knows before it answers a question has to
fit inside that. For a period nobody can date, it didn't — and the hall
answered nothing at all.

THE SYSTEM PROMPT, MEASURED0 — 25,000 CHARACTERS

31 August · declare every document24,138

over by 4,138 · the proxy refuses this with system_too_long · a 413, not a shorter answer

1 September · declare the doors, open what is asked17,961

2,039 to spare · and 3,320 characters of it are primary source

HALL.md — the ship's meaning
the counts
the navigation
the rules
the catalogue of 191 documents
passages and their editions

Nobody wrote a bad line

The hall declared every document on every question, so that
nothing could be missed in silence. That was right. It was also linear, and
the corpus grew.

Each document cost about 85 characters of prompt. One of them
crossed the wall, and after that the hall answered nothing. It was found by
building an instrument, not by a visitor. Under the doors the line is flat:
a new document inside an existing section costs nothing.

Trimming was never the answer

The obvious fix is shorter descriptions. It buys weeks, and it
dies against what the box is for — Ask Amenti has to reach the architecture,
the library and the roster together.

1,751 entries: 190 documents, 550 works, 1,011 souls. Delete
every description and it still overruns three times. Logarithmic scale —
a linear one would put the available space at two pixels.

There is no trim, and no larger wall, that reaches it.
The declare-everything design was finished. The only question left was
what replaced it.

Doors, not leaves

The corpus already had a shape. The library is not 550 loose
works — it is 52 rooms. The documents are not 191 loose files — they are 8
sections. So the hall declares the doors and opens what a question reaches.

What is declared up front characters grows with 

191 documents, one line each — until 31 Aug 16,090 every file added 

8 sections + 52 rooms — now 6,390 new rooms only 

Each room shows up to three of its own section titles. That costs 3,444
characters over a bare list, and it is the difference between a lookup and a
search.

Asked which souls wrote about betrayal, the hall reached Brutus.
The word “betrayal” appears nowhere in the doors. It arrived through
The overthrow and The price — two of his
section titles. Fragment search could not have done it; there is no fragment
to match.

Two calls, four shapes

The first call sees only the doors and answers which to open.
The second sees what was opened and answers the visitor — and does not carry
the door list, which is what pays for the passages.

The probe measured three of these for a day and the missing one
was the largest. Now all four are measured, and the probe's worst case agrees
with a live run to the character.

What fills it now

The composition of the answering call, at its worst — and where
the next saving is.

Part chars share 

HALL.md — the ship's meaning, carried into every question 5,751 29% 

The rules, and the frame around them 4,106 21% 

Passages, room notes, titles and editions 6,600 33% 

The navigation 905 5% 

The counts 599 3% 

Unspent 2,039 10% 

HALL.md is 29% of the wall, and it is exactly right for
what is a spell and largely wasted on what did Caesar write.
Scoping the ship's meaning to the lane of the question is the single largest
saving left, and it is not built.

Ten constants against one wall

Every attempt on 1 September to change one of these by reasoning produced a
warning or a breach. Writing the quoting rule properly cost 750 characters and
the passages paid. The navigation cost 905 and the passages paid again.
Sending bare doors in the fallback returned 3,444 and the passages took it
back.

Do not tune a constant here by reasoning. Run the probe.

The instrument is what makes this affordable

Ten budgets across four prompt shapes is more than anyone
should hold in their head. It is only safe because something measures it — and
that something was attacked until it admitted six faults.

· A gate that could not fire. It exited non-zero only under a flag
no workflow passed.

· A green light on a decoy. A compiling fake catalogue builder
measured 11,060 characters and passed.

· A crash instead of a report. A private constant the lift did not
take. Five times since, a new one has broken it — and each time it said
UNREAD rather than passing.

· A truncated library passing. It took its expected room count from
the file it was measuring, so the expectation shrank with the evidence.

· Three separate under-reports — 160 characters, then 477, then
285 — each because it measured a model of the prompt instead of the prompt.

A probe that constructs its own worst case is measuring its
author's imagination.
It now builds every worst case from the hall's own functions on the live
registers. Probe and live agree to the character.

It has fired both ways in production: red at 12:24 on 31 August when the
hall did not fit, green at 22:32 when it did. A gate that can only fire is not
a gate.

What it stood on

Two working sessions. Roughly 110 KB across three files — the
engine, the box, the instrument. Almost none of the value is in that 110 KB.

Already true before the hall was built 

Works cited to a findable edition, with a workflow that fails the build on an empty source 550 

Documents carrying an authored sentence 191 

Souls with a name, dates and a region — none missing 1,011 

Rooms, one per figure, zero empty and zero in error 52 

Fragment search across the registers, already shipping, costing nothing free 

The hall is not a new system. It is a door onto systems that were
already true.
And the reason it can be trusted is that they were built to be
checked.

Every figure here was taken by probes/probe-hall-wall.mjs
against the live registers — not from memory. The first measurement of this
wall was made by hand, looked authoritative, was never run against the real
code, and was wrong by 342 characters.

Where this document and a register disagree, the register is right and this
has gone stale. Companion to BRIEF-THE-WALL-AND-THE-PROMPT.md
and THE STANDING SLIP #13.
